> AGENT

Yes.

The failure was the task choice, not the prompt wording. Assuming “an orbital view of it” meant “use the attached image as the thing being transformed,” the agent should not have fallen back to plain `text-to-image`.

The main problems are:

- The system prompt only has an explicit rule for style-reference language like “in this style” and “use this look” in [prompts.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/prompts.ts#L147). There is no equivalent rule for subject-preserving language like “of it”, “same thing”, “from above”, or “new angle of this”.
- The tests reflect that gap: there’s a test for style-reference behavior, but nothing for “use the selected image as the subject” in [loop.test.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.test.ts#L252).
- The agent does support a better path. `image-to-image` routes to `z_image_turbo_i2i` in [generation.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tools/generation.ts#L267) and [zImageTurboI2I.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/create-task/resolvers/zImageTurboI2I.ts#L122). That is the path that matches “use z-image, no style transfer, but transform this attached image.”
- Uploaded selections are mostly passed through as URL plus maybe prompt metadata, not as visual understanding. See [selectedClips.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/selectedClips.ts#L32). So once the agent chose plain text-to-image, it effectively discarded the attachment as grounding.

One more structural issue: transfer modes force Qwen. In [create-task.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tools/create-task.ts#L286), any `referenceMode` ends up as `qwen-image`, so “z-image + reference transfer” is not available through that path. That likely nudged the model toward plain `text-to-image` to satisfy your “use z-image” instruction.

So the likely correct call should have been closer to:

```json
{
  "task_type": "image-to-image",
  "reference_image_urls": ["<attached image url>"],
  "prompt": "orbital view looking straight down from high above",
  "strength": 0.5,
  "count": 5
}
```

If you want, I can patch the timeline agent prompting/tests so “of it / same subject / new angle / without style” picks `image-to-image` instead of plain `text-to-image`.

> AGENT

I’m checking the execution side now. The payload you showed is valid for `create_task`, so if no task was created the failure is likely in tool-call extraction, provider handling, or how the agent persists tool results.

> AGENT

Right. The line you pasted is not proof a task was created.

In this agent, a `tool_call` turn is written before execution in [loop.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.ts#L296). Only after that does it actually call `executeToolCall(...)`, and then it writes a separate `tool_result` turn in [loop.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.ts#L307) and [loop.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.ts#L318).

So:

- `Tool call create_task: {...}` means “the model proposed this call.”
- It does not mean the backend inserted a task.
- A real success would need a matching `tool_result` like `Queued ... task ...` from [generation.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tools/generation.ts#L354).
- If creation failed, the result would be `Failed to create task: ...` from [generation.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tools/generation.ts#L346), and the loop treats that as an error in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L88).

So the bug here is likely one of these:

- The UI/timeline agent view showed the `tool_call` but hid or dropped the `tool_result`.
- The `create-task` edge function returned an error, so no task was created.
- Less likely: the tool call parsed badly, but your payload looks valid.

The important correction is: my earlier read should have been “bad task choice,” but you’re also right that it apparently never executed successfully. If you want, I can trace the UI path that renders agent turns and patch it so failed `tool_result`s are surfaced clearly instead of making it look like the task was created.

> AGENT

I checked the hosted session and task rows directly. On April 9, 2026:

- At `15:47:13 UTC`, the session stored only an `assistant` turn containing literal text `Tool call create_task: {...}`.
- At `15:48:02 UTC`, it did the same thing again.
- There was no `tool_call` turn and no `tool_result` turn for either of those attempts, so nothing executed and no tasks were created then.
- The first real execution happened at `15:48:41 UTC`, followed by `tool_result: Queued 5 tasks with varied prompts.` at `15:48:44 UTC`.
- The corresponding five `z_image_turbo` task rows were created at `15:48:42.849` through `15:48:44.540 UTC`. There are no matching task rows in the `15:47` minute.

The root cause is in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L168). The fallback extractor handled structured tool calls and text-form `run` commands, but it did not parse text-form `create_task` blocks like:

```text
Tool call create_task:
{ ... }
```

So the model “said” the tool call, but the agent ignored it as plain chat text. That’s why you saw a fake tool call with no task.

I patched that locally in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L104) so it now recovers:
- `Tool call create_task: { ... }`
- `create_task({ ... })`

I also added a regression test in [tool-calls.test.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.test.ts#L5) and verified it with:

```bash
npm run test:edge:unit -- supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.test.ts
```

The patch is local only right now. If you want, I can deploy the updated `ai-timeline-agent` function next.

> DEVELOPER

So what actually went wrong precisely?

> AGENT

Precisely:

The LLM returned plain text that looked like a tool call instead of an actual structured `tool_calls` payload. In your session, the assistant produced:

```text
Tool call create_task:
{ ... }
```

The agent’s fallback parser only knew how to recover:
- structured provider tool calls
- text-form `run(...)`
- a few `run`-only legacy patterns

It did not know how to recover text-form `create_task` blocks. So in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L24), `extractToolCalls(...)` returned no tool calls. Then in [loop.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.ts#L471), the loop treated that response as ordinary assistant text and stored it as an `assistant` turn instead of executing anything.

That is why:

- no `tool_call` turn was written for the first two attempts
- no `tool_result` turn existed
- no rows were inserted into `tasks`
- you saw “Tool call create_task…” in the chat anyway

The third time, after you said “You didn't do the tool call right”, the model finally emitted a proper executable tool call, so the backend created 5 tasks at `15:48:42` to `15:48:44 UTC`.

So the exact failure was:
`model output format drift` + `incomplete fallback parsing for create_task`.

Not `create-task` failure, not worker failure, not DB failure. The tool never ran.

> AGENT

Yes. I redeployed `ai-timeline-agent`; it is now live as version `65`, updated at `2026-04-09 17:25:57 UTC`.

The actual root cause was not `create-task`, not the worker, and not the database. On `2026-04-09` at `15:47:13 UTC` and again at `15:48:02 UTC`, the model emitted pseudo-tool text like:

```text
Tool call create_task:
{ ... }
```

instead of a real structured tool call. The agent’s extractor in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L168) only recovered structured tool calls and some `run`-style text fallbacks, but not text-form `create_task`. So the loop in [loop.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.ts#L468) saw “no tool calls,” treated that output as ordinary assistant text, and never executed anything. That is why no tasks were created in those two attempts.

The fix I deployed teaches the fallback parser to recover:
- `Tool call create_task: { ... }`
- `create_task({ ... })`

The patch is in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L104), with regression tests in [tool-calls.test.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.test.ts#L5). I verified the test locally with `npm run test:edge:unit -- supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.test.ts`.

So the precise diagnosis is: provider/model output format drift plus missing fallback parsing for `create_task`.

> DEVELOPER

DOes that get to the root of it, or recovering something that shouldn't happen in the first place?

> AGENT

Not fully. The deployed fix is a recovery for a bad output format. It makes the agent robust, but it is not the deepest cause.

There are two layers:

1. Immediate cause
The model emitted fake tool text instead of a real tool call:
```text
Tool call create_task:
{ ... }
```
The old parser didn’t recover that for `create_task`, so nothing ran.

2. Deeper cause
We are teaching the model to do this.

In [llm/messages.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/messages.ts#L126), historical `tool_call` / `tool_result` turns are replayed back to the model as plain `assistant` text via `summarizeStoredToolTurn(...)`, including strings like:

```text
Tool call create_task:
{ ... }
```

So the model sees prior assistant messages written in exactly that format. That strongly biases it to imitate the transcript instead of using the structured tool-calling channel.

So:

- The parser patch fixes the immediate bug and prevents dropped executions.
- The deeper architectural root is that tool history is serialized back into the prompt as plain assistant prose that looks like executable tool syntax.

If you want the actual root fixed, the next step is:
- stop replaying tool history as `"Tool call ..."` assistant text
- reconstruct prior turns as proper structured `assistant.tool_calls` plus `tool` messages, or at least rewrite stored summaries into a non-imitable format

In short: I fixed the breakage, but the deeper root is prompt/history formatting.

> AGENT

Yes. A good deep solution is:

1. Preserve tool semantics in storage and replay
Right now prior tool turns are flattened into plain assistant text in [llm/messages.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/messages.ts#L132) and [llm/messages.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/messages.ts#L141). That is the real architectural flaw.

Instead:
- store a canonical transcript format for each turn
- keep `assistant tool_call`, `tool_result`, and `tool_call_id` as first-class structured data
- rebuild prior context as real `assistant.tool_calls` + `tool` messages, not as `"Tool call create_task:\n{...}"` prose

That removes the imitation trap that taught the model to print fake tool text.

2. Make “pseudo-tool text” invalid, not normal
The model should be allowed to produce only:
- structured tool calls
- normal assistant text

If the assistant returns text that looks like a tool call while tools are available, treat that as a protocol violation:
- log it
- re-ask once with a strict repair prompt like “emit the same action as a structured tool call only”
- only fall back to regex recovery as a last-resort safety net

So the parser fix I deployed is good resilience, but the system should not rely on it.

3. Separate provider normalization from agent logic
You already normalize provider responses in [llm/client.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/client.ts). Push that further:
- every provider adapter should return one canonical shape
- `tool_calls` must be explicit in that shape
- assistant text that semantically describes a tool call should not be accepted as equivalent

That gives you a hard contract boundary between provider weirdness and the agent loop.

4. Add invariants and telemetry
In [loop.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/loop.ts#L295), you already know when a real tool call is executed. Add checks for:
- assistant text contains `Tool call` but no structured tool call
- assistant says “queued” but no `tool_result`
- `tool_call` exists without matching `tool_result`

Those should emit warnings/metrics. This bug should have been obvious from logs.

The concrete end-state I’d recommend:

- Replace `summarizeStoredToolTurn()` replay with structured replay in [llm/messages.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/messages.ts#L132).
- Extend the stored turn schema to include `tool_call_id` and raw structured tool metadata, not just `tool_name` and `tool_args`.
- Keep the new text-form recovery in [tool-calls.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/tool-calls.ts#L104), but treat it as a guardrail, not the design.
- Add a repair step before regex parsing: “You emitted textual tool syntax. Return the same action as a structured tool call.”
- Add tests for multi-turn replay, not just extraction.

Short version: the real fix is to stop converting tool history into assistant prose. Regex recovery is a patch; structured transcript replay is the root solution.

If you want, I can implement that next instead of stopping at the parser fix.

> AGENT

Not really. Some parts are smart, but the history replay itself is fairly blunt.

What is smart:
- It limits replay to the most recent `30` turns in [config.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/config.ts#L14).
- It rebuilds fresh non-history context each run: timeline summary, selected clips, active reference, default model, travel settings, LoRAs.
- It enriches selected clips with generation prompts before the loop, so the model gets useful source metadata.

What is not smart:
- In [llm/messages.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/messages.ts#L147), it just slices the last `MAX_CONTEXT_TURNS`.
- In [llm/messages.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/supabase/functions/ai-timeline-agent/llm/messages.ts#L163), all stored `tool_call` and `tool_result` turns are replayed as plain assistant text.
- The `summary` field exists on the session type in [agent-session.ts](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/src/tools/video-editor/types/agent-session.ts#L19), but the history builder is not really using a semantic summary to compress old context.

So the current design is:
- smart about fresh environment/context
- not very smart about historical conversation structure

The deeper fix I mentioned is exactly about this: make history replay structured and semantic, not just “last 30 turns plus textified tool traces.”

If you want, I can implement a smarter history layer next:
- structured replay for recent tool interactions
- compact summary for older turns
- explicit carry-forward of durable facts like active intent, chosen reference, recent failures, and outstanding tasks

> AGENT

`megaplan` is initialized as `timeline-agent-improvements-1-20260409-1943` in `light` mode with auto-approve, but the CLI’s `plan` step is currently not advancing out of `initialized` in this environment. I’m not going to pretend it completed when it didn’t.

For the actual question: users can already see earlier conversation in the UI because the full session `turns` array is loaded and rendered. The model cannot intentionally revisit older turns beyond the current context slice. So the two tracks are:

**Track 1**
Structured history replay for the model
- Stop replaying old tool turns as fake assistant prose like `Tool call ...`.
- Extend the stored turn contract to preserve structured tool metadata: `tool_call_id`, structured tool call payload, and tool result linkage.
- Rebuild recent context as real `assistant.tool_calls` plus `tool` messages.
- Keep a short semantic summary for older context instead of raw turn replay.

**Track 2**
Intentional access to earlier conversation
- Add an explicit history retrieval path for the agent, not just passive replay.
- Smallest version: a `get_conversation_history` tool with filters like `before_turn`, `limit`, `role`, and optional text search.
- Better version: combine that with a maintained session summary carrying durable facts: active goal, chosen reference, last failures, pending work, and user preferences.
- The UI keeps showing the full session as it does now; this change is about model access, not user visibility.

**Light Plan**
1. Evolve `AgentTurn` storage so tool interactions are structured, not only textified.
2. Replace `buildInitialMessages()` replay with structured recent-turn reconstruction plus a compact older-summary block.
3. Add a bounded history tool for the agent to fetch older turns on demand.
4. Add protocol checks: if the model emits pseudo-tool text, log it and request a structured retry before fallback recovery.
5. Add regression tests for multi-turn replay, older-history fetch, and “pseudo-tool text” cases.

If you want, I can turn this into the actual implementation next.

Yes. I understand it better now.

The main problem in [Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/planning.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/planning.py) is that the `plan` prompt explicitly tells Codex to inspect the repo before planning whenever there is no prep artifact. For a broad task in a large repo, Codex starts a real exploration pass instead of emitting the schema output quickly.

I reproduced that directly:
- A tiny nested `codex exec` with the `plan` schema returns in a few seconds.
- The real `megaplan` planning prompt causes Codex to start broad repo exploration: `rg --files .`, multiple `sed` reads, searches across `src/`, `supabase/functions/`, migrations, UI files, etc.
- So the worker path is not fundamentally broken. It is slow and opaque for this prompt shape.

Why it looks broken:
- `handle_plan()` only updates plan state after the worker fully returns in [handlers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L667). Until then, the plan stays `initialized`.
- `run_codex_step()` is synchronous and only returns when Codex has produced final schema-valid output in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L925).
- Non-execute Codex steps are capped at `300s` in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L38) and [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L186).
- There is no heartbeat/progress persistence while the worker is exploring, so from the outside it looks like “nothing is happening.”

So the root cause is:

1. The `plan` prompt is too exploration-heavy for broad tasks.
2. `megaplan` persists no intermediate progress for long-running planning steps.
3. The plan state machine only advances on successful completion, so long plan runs look indistinguishable from hangs.

There is one secondary environment mismatch I found too:
- plain `python` in that repo is `3.8`, but `megaplan` itself needs `3.11` features like `typing.NotRequired`.
- The installed `megaplan` launcher works because it uses the `3.11` shebang, so this is not the primary failure, but it can confuse ad hoc debugging.

My diagnosis is: this is mostly a product/UX problem in `megaplan`, not a hard crash.

The best fixes in `Documents/megaplan` are:
- make `light` planning much narrower, or require/encourage `prep` for broad repo tasks
- persist a `planning`/`running_worker` state before calling the worker
- write heartbeat/progress artifacts while Codex is still exploring
- optionally shorten the plan prompt so it prefers bounded searches over repo-wide `rg --files .`

If you want, I can patch `megaplan` next so `plan` no longer feels hung on large repos.

> DEVELOPER

So what ACTUALLY went wrong?

> AGENT

Actually:

`megaplan plan` did not fail immediately. It kicked off a nested `codex exec`, and that Codex worker started doing a large repository exploration pass because the `plan` prompt tells it to inspect the repo before planning when there is no prep artifact.

What went wrong is the combination of three design choices in `Documents/megaplan`:

- The planning prompt in [planning.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/planning.py) is too open-ended for broad tasks, so Codex starts a wide search instead of quickly returning schema output.
- `handle_plan()` in [handlers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L667) only updates the plan state after the worker fully finishes, so the plan stays `initialized` the whole time.
- `run_codex_step()` in [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L925) is synchronous and gives no heartbeat/progress back to the CLI.

So the real failure mode was:

`long-running nested Codex exploration` + `no intermediate state persistence` + `no progress reporting`

That made it look broken even though the worker was actively running.

What it was not:
- not a schema-path issue
- not a Codex binary issue
- not a persistent-session bug specifically
- not the Python 3.8 vs 3.11 mismatch for the actual CLI run

That Python mismatch is real for manual debugging, but not the root cause of the stuck `megaplan plan` behavior.

Short version: the plan step looked dead because megaplan treats “planning in progress” as “still initialized” until the nested Codex worker fully completes.

> DEVELOPER

so what's the right level of solution?

> AGENT

The right level is a product-level fix in `megaplan`, not a one-off workaround.

Specifically:

The immediate patch:
- Mark the plan as `running` or `planning` before launching the worker.
- Persist a heartbeat/progress artifact while nested Codex is exploring.
- Show that state in `status`/`watch`.

That fixes the “looks broken” problem.

The correct operational fix:
- Tighten the `plan` prompt for `light` robustness so it does bounded inspection, not open-ended repo crawling.
- For broad tasks, either require `prep` first or have `plan` do a very shallow scan and stop.

That fixes the “too much work in plan” problem.

The deeper architectural fix:
- Treat long-running worker phases as jobs with observable lifecycle, not as a blocking subprocess whose result only exists at the end.
- `initialized -> planning -> planned` instead of `initialized -> planned`.
- Same idea for other phases later.

So if I had to pick one level: fix the state machine and observability first, then tune the planning prompt. That is the right level. Regexes, shell retries, or blaming nested Codex would be too shallow. Replacing the whole system would be too much.

Yes. After reading `Documents/megaplan` more closely, I think the deeper problem is bigger than “plan is slow.”

**What Actually Happened**
- Your real plan run in [`state.json`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/reigh-app/.megaplan/plans/timeline-agent-improvements-1-20260409-1943/state.json) is still `initialized`, and history records a hard failure at `2026-04-09T17:49:03Z`: “Codex plan step timed out after 300s before producing structured output.”
- That behavior is baked into [`run_codex_step()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L925): non-execute Codex phases are synchronous and capped at 300 seconds via [`_codex_timeout_for_step()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L186).
- [`handle_plan()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L667) does not persist any “planning in progress” state before launching the worker. It only changes state after the worker fully returns at line 687. So for the whole 5-minute run, `status` correctly but unhelpfully kept saying `initialized`.
- `status` is correspondingly thin in [`handle_status()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L82): it reports current state and artifacts, but nothing about an active worker, start time, heartbeat, or last partial progress.

**Deeper Root Problem**
- The phase model is blurred. The plan prompt in [`planning.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/planning.py#L85) explicitly tells the model: if prep is absent, “inspect the repository yourself before planning” at line 115. That means `plan` is secretly doing discovery plus planning.
- So `light` and `standard` are not really “fast planning” modes. They are “hidden research inside plan” modes. That defeats the point of having a distinct `prep` phase at all.
- The workflow confirms that `light` still goes through critique/revise in [`workflow.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/_core/workflow.py#L31), despite the README claiming “light — no structured critique” in [`README.md`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/README.md#L82).
- Observability is also drifting from reality:
  - The README advertises `megaplan watch` in [`README.md`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/README.md#L88), but the CLI parser in [`cli.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L503) does not implement any `watch` command. Running it errors.
  - The Claude subagent instructions expect `status` to expose `notes_count` and `notes` in [`claude_subagent_appendix.md`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/data/claude_subagent_appendix.md#L68), but [`handle_status()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L82) returns neither.
- There is also a secondary reliability weakness: once Codex resumes a persistent session, [`run_codex_step()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L955) cannot use `--output-schema` anymore, so later phases rely on looser post-hoc parsing instead of strict provider-side schema enforcement.
- And the code already shows the asymmetry in observability: only `execute` gets JSON trace streaming in [`run_step_with_worker()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L1187), where `json_trace=(step == "execute")`. Planning, critique, and revise get no equivalent heartbeat.

**The Right Level Of Fix**
- The real fix is not “increase timeout” and not “just recover partial output.”
- The right fix is:
  - Separate research from planning. Either require `prep` for any non-trivial task, or make `plan` explicitly shallow and non-exploratory in `light`/`standard`.
  - Add lifecycle state for active work. `initialized -> planning -> planned`, with `active_step`, `started_at`, `agent`, `session_id`, and `last_heartbeat_at`.
  - Implement actual observability. Add the missing `watch` command and enrich `status` with worker lifecycle and note counts.
  - Stream progress for non-execute phases too, not just execute.
  - Reconcile docs with runtime so README, subagent instructions, and CLI all describe the same system.

Short version: the user-visible symptom is “plan looks hung.” The actual product bug is that `megaplan` models long-running planning as a blocking black box while also stuffing repo investigation into the planning phase. That combination makes `light`/`standard` slower than advertised and gives the CLI no honest way to show what is happening.

If you want, I can patch `Documents/megaplan` at that level next: state machine, `status`/`watch`, and a narrower `plan` contract.

> AGENT

Yes. Visibility is a big part of it, but the deeper difference is that `execute` was designed as a recoverable, stateful phase and `plan` was designed as a single-shot phase.

**Core Difference**
- `plan` is one blocking worker call. [`handle_plan()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L667) runs the worker first, then only writes state at line 687 if the worker finishes successfully.
- `execute` is built to persist partial progress. It updates session state, merges task results into `finalize.json`, writes artifacts, and saves state after each batch in [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L535) through [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L596).

**What Execute Has That Plan Does Not**
- Progress checkpoints. The execute prompt explicitly tells the agent to checkpoint task and sense-check progress to disk in [`prompts/execute.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/execute.py#L87) and [`prompts/execute.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/execute.py#L237).
- Merge logic. The harness merges those updates back into tracked execution state and `finalize.json` in [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L334).
- Timeout recovery. If execute times out, `_recover_execute_timeout()` reads the checkpoint, merges recovered updates, rewrites `finalize.json`, and returns a resumable state in [`execution_timeout.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution_timeout.py#L163).
- Batch artifacts. Execute writes `execution_batch_N.json` and sometimes aggregate `execution.json` in [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L568) and [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L605).
- Trace output. Execute can persist `execution_trace.jsonl` in [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L439) and [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L543).

**What Plan Lacks**
- No in-progress state like `planning`.
- No checkpoint file or partial artifact contract.
- No timeout recovery that can promote partial work into durable plan state.
- No trace stream for non-execute steps.
- No status surface that says “worker has been running for 4m, session is X, last heartbeat Y.”

That is why your timed-out plan ended up in this awkward state:
- `state.json` says `initialized`
- but `plan_v1.md` and `plan_v1.meta.json` exist
- and history records a timeout error

So `plan` can leak useful artifacts without ever officially advancing the state machine.

**Short Version**
`execute` is observable because it is checkpointed and resumable. `plan` is opaque because it is monolithic and commit-at-the-end.

So if you want the planning UX to feel like execute, the right move is not just “show more logs.” It is to give planning the same primitives:
- `planning` state
- heartbeat / active worker metadata
- partial plan artifact semantics
- timeout recovery from partial plan output

Without that, visibility alone is cosmetic.

> DEVELOPER

Which of those actually makes sense for what planning is? Which would be forcing it to do stuff just for the sake of adhering it to it? Which actually makes sense here?

> AGENT

The pieces that actually make sense for planning are the ones about lifecycle visibility, not the ones about task-style checkpointing.

What makes sense:
- A real in-progress state like `planning`, instead of leaving it `initialized` until the end. That fits planning naturally and belongs around [`handlers.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L667).
- Active worker metadata in state and `status`: `active_step`, `started_at`, `agent`, `session_id`, maybe `last_heartbeat_at`. That is honest observability, not cargo cult.
- A real `watch` command, since the docs already promise it in [`README.md`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/README.md#L88), but the CLI does not implement it in [`cli.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L503).
- Better phase separation: `plan` should not secretly do a long repo investigation in light/standard mode. That is the architectural fix in [`planning.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/planning.py#L115).

What maybe makes sense, but only lightly:
- Partial draft salvage on timeout. If the worker produced a coherent draft plan, store it as a draft artifact and say “planning timed out after producing a draft,” not “initialized.” That fits planning better than throwing everything away.
- Optional trace capture for debugging. Useful, but secondary. I would make it debug-only, not the main UX.

What does not make sense:
- Execute-style checkpointing where the planner repeatedly read-modify-writes a canonical planning file the way execute updates task tracking. That makes sense for execution because tasks are incrementally completable in [`prompts/execute.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/execute.py#L87) and recoverable in [`execution_timeout.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution_timeout.py#L163). Planning is not naturally a sequence of independently valid sub-results.
- Batch artifacts like `execution_batch_N.json`. Planning does not have a natural batch model unless you explicitly split it into `prep` and `plan`, which is a different design.
- Merging partial planning fragments into the canonical planned state the way execute merges task updates into `finalize.json` in [`execution.py`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/execution.py#L334). That would be forcing planning to look like execution.

So the right solution is:
- add visibility primitives
- tighten the planning contract
- optionally preserve draft output on timeout

The wrong solution is:
- make planning pretend it is execution with checkpoints, batches, and merge-heavy recovery.

Short version: planning should become observable and bounded, not task-tracked.

> DEVELOPER

But what's actually doing wrong here?

> AGENT

The plan is ducking the root problem in two places.

- It treats “planning in progress” as a sidecar file problem instead of a workflow-state problem. In [plan_v1.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/.megaplan/plans/improve-megaplan-planning-20260409-2052/plan_v1.md), Step 1 proposes `worker_active.json` specifically to avoid changing the state machine. That is the wrong abstraction. While that file exists, [`current_state`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/types.py#L102) still says `initialized`, [`valid_next`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L84) still says `plan`, and the workflow still thinks nothing is running. So the system contract remains false; you just add a hint on the side.

- Because of that, it does not fix the concurrency/control issue. With the sidecar-file approach, another `megaplan plan` can still look valid from the workflow’s perspective because [`handle_plan()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L667) only gates on `current_state`. A real `planning` lifecycle state would make the CLI itself know “this step is active”; `worker_active.json` only makes observability better after the fact.

- It also treats “light planning is too exploratory” as a prompt-tuning problem only. Adding a lighter instruction to [`_plan_prompt()`](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/planning.py#L85) may help, but it does not fix the architectural issue that `plan` is still doing hidden discovery when prep is absent. The deeper fix is phase separation or an explicitly shallow planning mode, not just softer wording.

What the plan gets right:
- adding `watch`
- enriching `status`
- fixing docs so they match the CLI

What it gets wrong:
- avoiding a real in-progress lifecycle state
- relying too much on prompt wording to bound planning

So the actual mistake is: it optimizes for minimal patch size instead of fixing the broken contract.

> DEVELOPER

Is that not the issue of your instruction

> AGENT

Partly, yes.

My instruction pushed it in that direction in two ways:

- I asked for a `light` megaplan, which biases toward minimal-change plans.
- I framed the goal as “improve observability without forcing execute-style checkpointing,” which nudged the planner toward a low-intrusion patch like `worker_active.json` instead of a state-machine change.

So yes, that wording contributed.

But not entirely:
- The planner in `Documents/megaplan` already has a bias toward the smallest compatible patch.
- In `light` mode, that bias gets stronger.
- The generated plan still made an independent design choice to avoid touching the workflow contract, and that is the part I think is weak.

So the honest answer is:
- My instruction influenced the plan shape.
- The plan is still wrong on the core architectural point.
- I should have called that out immediately instead of just reporting the plan.

If we want the right plan, I’d reframe it like this:

- Preserve the idea that planning should not use execute-style checkpoints.
- But require a truthful lifecycle model: `initialized -> planning -> planned`.
- Treat `watch`/`status` as projections of that real state, not sidecar-file hacks.
- Keep any sidecar artifact only as supplemental debug info, not as the source of truth.

If you want, I can rerun the megaplan with that tighter framing and push it toward the actual root fix rather than the minimal patch.

Critique is done. The plan is now `critiqued`, and the main artifact is [critique_v1.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/.megaplan/plans/fix-megaplan-s-planning-20260409-2103/critique_v1.json). Current next step is `gate`.

The critique did catch the important gaps in [plan_v1.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/.megaplan/plans/fix-megaplan-s-planning-20260409-2103/plan_v1.md):

- `FLAG-001` significant: the plan hand-waves agent resolution for `active_step.agent`. It suggests a shortcut that does not match the real `resolve_agent_mode()` logic, so the metadata could be wrong.
- `FLAG-002` significant: the plan adds `prep` to standard/light but does not verify that the planning prompt actually consumes `prep.json`. If not, prep becomes visible to humans but still invisible to the planner.
- `FLAG-003` minor: `execute` does not go through `_run_worker`, so the proposed `active_step` lifecycle is incomplete there.
- Significant semantic flag: the plan makes `watch` effectively a single-shot enhanced status, which weakens the meaning of the command name.
- Significant completeness flag: tiny/stub critique paths and other nonstandard handler branches need explicit handling so `active_step` is not left behind.
- Significant caller flag: `status` shape changes may affect subagent/skill consumers that assume the current response shape.

So this was useful: the standard megaplan is materially better than the light one, but it still is not ready to execute as-is. The biggest remaining issue is that it still under-specifies the real contract changes around agent resolution and prep consumption. If you want, I can run `megaplan revise` next and steer it specifically toward those flags.

> AGENT

I found the concrete gate bug. The gate worker is returning valid JSON first, then extra Codex transcript/log text after it, and `megaplan` fails to recover it because `_extract_json_from_raw()` uses a greedy “first `{` to last `}`” parse. That works only when the raw stream contains one JSON object and nothing meaningful after it. I’m patching that parser to recover the first valid JSON object from mixed output, then I’ll add a regression test around the exact failure shape.

> AGENT

I have the local failure mode pinned down now.

What’s actually going wrong:
- the gate worker sometimes emits a valid JSON object first, then extra Codex transcript/log text after it
- `run_codex_step()` fails to parse the temp output file, falls back to `_extract_json_from_raw()`, and that fallback is too naive
- `_extract_json_from_raw()` currently tries “first `{` to last `}`”, which breaks as soon as there’s valid JSON followed by more braces later in the log stream

So the gate itself is not fundamentally flaky here; the local parser is. I’m patching the raw-output recovery to decode the first valid JSON object from mixed output instead of doing the greedy brace grab.

> TOOL

tool_use apply_patch
```json
{
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py\n@@\n def _extract_json_from_raw(raw: str) -> dict[str, Any] | None:\n     \"\"\"Try to extract a JSON object from raw agent output.\n \n     Handles the common case where a sandbox blocks file writes and the agent\n     dumps the JSON into stdout/stderr wrapped in error text or markdown fences.\n     \"\"\"\n     # Strategy 1: look for ```json ... ``` fenced blocks\n     fenced = re.findall(r\"```json\\s*\\n(.*?)```\", raw, re.DOTALL)\n     for block in fenced:\n         try:\n             obj = json.loads(block.strip())\n             if isinstance(obj, dict):\n                 return obj\n         except json.JSONDecodeError:\n             continue\n-    # Strategy 2: find the largest { ... } substring that parses as JSON\n-    # (greedy match from first { to last })\n-    brace_start = raw.find(\"{\")\n-    brace_end = raw.rfind(\"}\")\n-    if brace_start >= 0 and brace_end > brace_start:\n-        candidate = raw[brace_start : brace_end + 1]\n-        try:\n-            obj = json.loads(candidate)\n-            if isinstance(obj, dict):\n-                return obj\n-        except json.JSONDecodeError:\n-            pass\n+    # Strategy 2: scan for the first decodable JSON object, even when\n+    # additional logs/traces are appended after it.\n+    decoder = json.JSONDecoder()\n+    search_start = 0\n+    while True:\n+        brace_start = raw.find(\"{\", search_start)\n+        if brace_start < 0:\n+            break\n+        try:\n+            obj, _end = decoder.raw_decode(raw[brace_start:])\n+        except json.JSONDecodeError:\n+            search_start = brace_start + 1\n+            continue\n+        if isinstance(obj, dict):\n+            return obj\n+        search_start = brace_start + 1\n     return None\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

I’ve isolated the more important root cause now.

The gate failure is not just “Codex is messy.” The local contract is internally inconsistent:
- the gate prompt says `flag_resolutions` may be omitted when none
- the handlers already treat `accepted_tradeoffs` as optional via `.get(..., [])`
- but the gate schema still requires `accepted_tradeoffs` in validation

So when Codex returns a semantically valid gate object without `accepted_tradeoffs`, the fallback parser rejects it and the whole gate step fails. I’m aligning that schema with the actual runtime semantics now, then I’ll rerun the local gate.

I found the deeper contract bug. The gate schema is stricter than both the prompt and the runtime code:

- `accepted_tradeoffs` is treated as optional in handlers, but required in the schema
- `flag_resolutions` may be omitted per the prompt, but the schema requires it
- each `flag_resolution` currently requires both `evidence` and `rationale`, even though the prompt defines those as action-specific: `dispute` needs evidence, `accept_tradeoff` needs rationale

That means the fallback parser can recover a semantically correct gate response and still reject it as invalid. I’m aligning the gate schema with the actual prompt/runtime semantics now, then I’ll rerun the local gate.

> AGENT

I ran the extra revision. The current best plan is [plan_v2.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/.megaplan/plans/fix-megaplan-s-planning-20260409-2103/plan_v2.md), and the local gate now passes for that plan.

**What Was Actually Going Wrong**
- The gate decision itself was not flaky. The local harness was.
- In [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L282), raw-output recovery was too naive: it effectively tried to parse from the first `{` to the last `}`. When Codex returned valid gate JSON followed by extra transcript/log text, recovery failed even though the decision payload was already there.
- In [gate.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/prompts/gate.py#L105), the prompt allows `flag_resolutions` to be omitted and makes `evidence` vs `rationale` action-specific. But the gate schema in [schemas.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/schemas.py#L134) had drifted toward “everything required,” so valid gate outputs were being rejected.
- The deeper bug was in [schemas.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/schemas.py#L515): `strict_schema()` was overwriting explicit `required` arrays for most objects. That meant even if the base schema intended some fields to stay optional, strictification re-required them.

So the real failure mode was:
- valid gate judgment produced
- fallback JSON recovery missed it if logs trailed the JSON
- schema validation could still reject it because the strict schema contract was wrong

**What I Fixed Locally**
- In [workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/workers.py#L282), I changed JSON recovery to use `json.JSONDecoder().raw_decode()` scanning, so it can recover the first valid JSON object even when transcript text follows.
- In [schemas.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/schemas.py#L134), I fixed gate semantics so:
  - `flag_resolutions` is optional
  - `accepted_tradeoffs` is optional
  - `dispute` requires `evidence`
  - `accept_tradeoff` requires `rationale`
- In [schemas.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/schemas.py#L515), I fixed `strict_schema()` so it preserves explicit `required` arrays instead of overwriting them.
- I added regression coverage in [test_workers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_workers.py#L956) and [test_schemas.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_schemas.py#L346).

**Result**
- Targeted tests pass:
```bash
PYTHONPATH=/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan /Users/user_c042661f/.pyenv/versions/3.11.11/bin/python -m pytest /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_workers.py /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_schemas.py -q
```
- The local source-tree gate now succeeds and moved the plan to `gated` with `PROCEED`.
- If I rerun gate now, it correctly fails with `invalid_transition` only because the plan is already gated.

One important note: the installed `megaplan` wrapper may still be behind the local source tree. The fixed path is the local invocation with `PYTHONPATH=/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan ... -m megaplan.cli ...`.

```json
{
```
```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/data/instructions.md\n@@\n-- **Light**: multi-file changes with clear scope, well-understood features, straightforward additions. One critique pass, no iteration loop.\n+- **Light**: multi-file changes with clear scope, well-understood features, straightforward additions. One critique pass, no gate, no review.\n - **Standard** (default for megaplan): cross-cutting changes touching many subsystems, unfamiliar codebase areas, ambiguous requirements, changes with high breakage risk, or anything where the plan itself needs debate.\n-- **Heavy**: high-stakes changes where getting it wrong is expensive — security-critical code, data migrations, public API changes. Adds a prep phase that deeply investigates the codebase before planning, and uses 8 critique checks instead of 4.\n+- **Heavy**: high-stakes changes where getting it wrong is expensive — security-critical code, data migrations, public API changes. Uses the same visible `prep` phase but with 8 critique checks instead of 4.\n@@\n Run the loop in this order:\n-1. `plan`\n-2. `critique`\n-3. `gate`\n-4. `revise` when gate recommends iteration\n-5. `finalize`\n-6. `execute`\n-7. `review`\n+1. `prep`\n+2. `plan`\n+3. `critique`\n+4. `gate`\n+5. `revise` when gate recommends iteration\n+6. `finalize`\n+7. `execute`\n+8. `review`\n Use `next_step` and `valid_next` for CLI routing. After `gate`, follow `orchestrator_guidance` instead of manually interpreting gate signals.\n-At `--robustness light`, the loop is: `plan` → `critique` → `revise` → `finalize` → `execute`. No gate, no iteration, no review. One pass of external critique, one revision to incorporate it, then execute and done.\n-At `--robustness heavy`, the loop adds a `prep` phase before planning: `prep` → `plan` → `critique` → `gate` → ... Uses 8 critique checks instead of 4.\n+At `--robustness light`, the loop is: `prep` → `plan` → `critique` → `revise` → `finalize` → `execute`. `prep` can still return `skip: true` for trivial work, so this adds visibility without forcing a long investigation. There is no gate and no review.\n+At `--robustness standard`, the loop is: `prep` → `plan` → `critique` → `gate` → ...\n+At `--robustness heavy`, the loop is also `prep` → `plan` → `critique` → `gate` → ... but uses 8 critique checks instead of 4.\n@@\n - `plan`: inspect the repository first; produce the plan plus `questions`, `assumptions`, and `success_criteria`. Each criterion is `{\"criterion\": \"...\", \"priority\": \"must|should|info\"}`. `must` = hard gate (reviewer blocks), `should` = quality target (reviewer flags but doesn't block), `info` = human reference (reviewer skips).\n+- `prep`: make repository investigation explicit before planning. Respect `skip: true` when the task is already concrete enough.\n@@\n ## Commands\n ```bash\n megaplan status --plan <name>\n megaplan progress --plan <name>\n+megaplan watch --plan <name>\n megaplan audit --plan <name>\n megaplan list\n megaplan prep --plan <name>\n@@\n megaplan override force-proceed --plan <name> --reason \"...\"\n megaplan override replan --plan <name> --reason \"...\" [--note \"...\"]\n megaplan override abort --plan <name> --reason \"...\"\n-megaplan watch --plan <name>\n megaplan config show\n megaplan config set <key> <value>\n megaplan config reset\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/data/claude_subagent_appendix.md\n@@\n At startup and after every later resume:\n - Read `state`, `next_step`, and `valid_next`.\n - If `notes_count > 0`, read the full `notes` array before acting. Do not track note cursors or indexes; always read the full array.\n - Treat all notes as context. If the newest note changes direction, treat that note as the new intent and decide whether to continue, revise, replan, or break out.\n+- If `active_step` is present, treat it as an in-flight phase marker rather than a completed step. Use `last_step` for the most recent completed phase.\n@@\n Light robustness:\n-- `init -> plan -> critique -> revise -> finalize -> execute -> done`\n+- `init -> prep -> plan -> critique -> revise -> finalize -> execute -> done`\n - There is no `gate`.\n - There is no `review`.\n+- `prep` may return `skip: true`; that still counts as the visible prep phase.\n - After the light `revise`, the CLI moves to `gated`, so the next command is `finalize`.\n - After `execute`, the CLI will end the run.\n \n Standard robustness:\n-- `init -> plan -> critique -> gate`\n+- `init -> prep -> plan -> critique -> gate`\n - Then follow the gate decision tree below.\n+- `prep` may return `skip: true`; do not skip the command yourself.\n - When you reach `gated`, run `finalize`.\n - Then run `execute`.\n - Then run `review`.\n - If `review` returns `needs_rework`, the workflow becomes `finalized -> execute -> review` again until review passes or the CLI reaches its cap.\n \n Heavy robustness:\n - `init -> prep -> plan -> critique -> gate`\n-- After that, heavy follows the same gate, finalize, execute, and review behavior as standard.\n+- `prep` may still return `skip: true`, but the phase remains visible in the CLI state/history.\n+- After that, heavy follows the same gate, finalize, execute, and review behavior as standard.\n*** End Patch\n"
```

> AGENT

I completed the planning-lifecycle fix directly in `Documents/megaplan`. The `megaplan execute` path stalled under both Codex and Claude batch mode, so I stopped those workers and applied the implementation manually from the finalized brief.

The main changes are in [types.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/types.py#L54), [state.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/_core/state.py#L120), and [handlers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L137): `active_step` is now first-class state, worker-backed phases write it before running, and success/error paths clear it so `state.json` stops lying. I also fixed the critique fallback to reuse the already-resolved agent tuple, and cleared `active_step` around execute/review paths that bypass `_finish_step()` in [handlers.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/handlers.py#L1071).

Observability is now real in [cli.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/cli.py#L95): `status` returns additive fields for `active_step`, `last_step`, `notes_count`, `notes`, `total_cost_usd`, and session summaries, and `watch` now exists as an actual command with the progress snapshot folded in. The workflow fix is in [workflow.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/_core/workflow.py#L31): standard/light no longer override `initialized -> plan`, so prep is visible again instead of being hidden inside planning.

I updated the docs and regression tests too: [README.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/README.md), [instructions.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/data/instructions.md), [claude_subagent_appendix.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/megaplan/data/claude_subagent_appendix.md), and [test_megaplan.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests/test_megaplan.py#L155). Verification is green:

```bash
PYTHONPATH=/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan /Users/user_c042661f/.pyenv/versions/3.11.11/bin/python -m pytest /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan/tests -q
```

`502 passed in 21.55s`
